# Loose ends, fix 2 - the distractor sweep

Mide's ruling: a practice option must not carry its mistake or working in its label. The label is the text before the first spaced em-dash; the reason moves into the feedback. Generated by `tools/option_labels_sweep.py` from the built site and the one rule in `option_labels.py`; regenerate after any content change.

## 1. Mechanism

One display-time rule (`option_labels.plan`), applied where the options are drawn - stored data, mirrors, knowledge points and shuffle seeds are untouched, and the shuffle is still seeded from the ORIGINAL text so no option moves.

| renderer | file | where the reason goes |
|---|---|---|
| KS4 topic-page quiz (4 emitters) | `generate_site_v5.py` | a wrong option with no authored explanation gets its reason as the wrong-answer feedback; a correct option's reason has no slot on these pages (the page says only "Correct") and is dropped from the label |
| KS4 lesson pages (pilot + batches; `K.find`, `K.bank`, inline `V()`/`Q()`) | `shared/ks4-lib.js` `item()`, text written by `ks4_rulings.py` R21 from `option_labels.js_source()` | wrong option: authored explanation, else the reason; correct option: the standard line plus the reason |
| KS3 mastery ladder | `build_ks3.py` `_rung_marked` | wrong option: authored correction, else the reason; a correct option's reason has no slot (the page says only "Correct.") and is dropped from the label |

A question is left EXACTLY as authored (and listed in section 3) when stripping would damage it: an empty or bare-connective head, two options collapsing to the same label, a pairing/matching stem, or a short head-plus-descriptor pair.

## 2. Summary

| corpus | questions with a reason | stripped | option labels shortened | exceptions | pages affected |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| KS3 ladder | 134 | 124 | 209 | 10 | 104 |
| KS4 lesson inline | 255 | 241 | 611 | 14 | 133 |
| KS4 biology | 190 | 185 | 368 | 5 | 76 |
| KS4 chemistry | 197 | 182 | 561 | 15 | 191 |
| KS4 physics | 161 | 157 | 552 | 4 | 222 |
| **total** | 937 | 889 | 2301 | 48 | 726 (distinct) |

KS4 counts are per DISTINCT question (the same question on several routes counts once); page counts are distinct URLs where a stripped question actually renders in the current build. 0 inline V()/Q() evaluation errors.

Not counted above: 290 unscored KS3 activity options on 97 pages (section 7) - not changed, ruling needed.

### Exception detail

**1. KS4 biology/bioenergetics/anaerobic-respiration** `duplicate-labels` - Which process produces more ATP per glucose molecule?
    - [x] `Aerobic respiration — approximately 36–38 ATP compared to 2 ATP from anaerobic`
    - [ ] `Anaerobic respiration — it happens faster so produces more total ATP`
    - [ ] `They produce the same amount — both start with glucose`
    - [ ] `Anaerobic respiration — the lactic acid contains extra energy`
    - pages: not rendered

**2. KS4 biology/bioenergetics/photosynthesis** `duplicate-labels` - In a dark room, does a plant carry out photosynthesis?
    - [x] `No — photosynthesis requires light energy and cannot occur in darkness`
    - [ ] `Yes — plants always photosynthesise, day and night`
    - [ ] `Yes — plants store light energy during the day and use it at night`
    - [ ] `Only partially — stomata close so photosynthesis slows but doesn't stop`
    - pages: not rendered

**3. KS4 biology/ecology/abiotic-biotic-factors** `duplicate-labels` - Grey squirrels were introduced to the UK and have outcompeted red squirrels. What type of factor is this?
    - [x] `Biotic — interspecific competition between two different species for food and habitat`
    - [ ] `Abiotic — grey squirrels changed the temperature of the habitat`
    - [ ] `Biotic — intraspecific competition between individuals of the same species`
    - [ ] `Abiotic — grey squirrels are larger so they change the physical environment`
    - pages: not rendered

**4. KS4 biology/ecology/food-chains-webs** `duplicate-labels` - In a food web, the rabbit population suddenly increases due to a disease killing foxes. What happens to the grass?
    - [x] `Grass decreases — more rabbits eat more grass`
    - [ ] `Grass increases — without foxes, more nutrients return to soil`
    - [ ] `Grass is unaffected — plants are not part of the animal food web`
    - [ ] `Grass increases — rabbits and grass have a mutualistic relationship`
    - pages: not rendered

**5. KS4 biology/homeostasis/defects-of-the-eye** `duplicate-labels` - A student cannot see the board clearly but can read their textbook easily. What is the most likely condition and correction?
    - [x] `Short-sight (myopia) — corrected with concave (diverging) lenses`
    - [ ] `Long-sight (hyperopia) — corrected with convex (converging) lenses`
    - [ ] `Short-sight (myopia) — corrected with convex (converging) lenses`
    - [ ] `Long-sight (hyperopia) — corrected with concave (diverging) lenses`
    - pages: not rendered

**6. KS4 chemistry/bonding/chemical-bonds** `duplicate-labels` - Water (H₂O) is made from two non-metals. Predict the type of bonding it contains.
    - [x] `Covalent — the non-metal atoms share pairs of electrons`
    - [ ] `Ionic — one atom transfers electrons to the other`
    - [ ] `Metallic — the atoms share a sea of delocalised electrons`
    - [ ] `Ionic — because water can conduct electricity`
    - pages: /combined/foundation/chemistry/bonding/chemical-bonds.html, /combined/higher/chemistry/bonding/chemical-bonds.html, /triple/foundation/chemistry/bonding/chemical-bonds.html, /triple/higher/chemistry/bonding/chemical-bonds.html

**7. KS4 chemistry/bonding/giant-covalent-structures** `duplicate-labels` - State whether diamond conducts electricity, and give a reason.
    - [x] `No — all four outer electrons of each carbon are used in bonds, so none are free to move`
    - [ ] `Yes — like graphite, each of its carbons has a delocalised outer electron that is free to move`
    - [ ] `Yes — it is made of carbon, and every form of carbon conducts electricity like graphite does`
    - [ ] `Only when molten — its carbon ions become free to move once the solid lattice melts`
    - pages: /triple/foundation/chemistry/bonding/giant-covalent-structures.html

**8. KS4 chemistry/bonding/ionic-compounds** `duplicate-labels` - Predict whether solid potassium bromide will conduct electricity, and give a reason.
    - [x] `No — its ions are fixed in the lattice and cannot move`
    - [ ] `Yes — it contains ions, which always conduct`
    - [ ] `Yes — its delocalised electrons carry the current`
    - [ ] `No — potassium bromide contains no charged particles`
    - pages: /combined/foundation/chemistry/bonding/ionic-compounds.html, /triple/foundation/chemistry/bonding/ionic-compounds.html

**9. KS4 chemistry/bonding/ionic-compounds** `duplicate-labels` - Predict, with reasons, whether calcium oxide (CaO) or potassium chloride (KCl) has the higher melting point.
    - [x] `CaO — its ions carry 2+ and 2− charges, so the electrostatic forces are stronger than in KCl (1+/1−) and more energy is needed to melt it`
    - [ ] `KCl — potassium is a larger atom, which always raises the melting point`
    - [ ] `They are equal, because both are ionic compounds`
    - [ ] `KCl — it has more ions in its formula`
    - pages: /triple/higher/chemistry/bonding/ionic-compounds.html

**13. KS4 chemistry/bonding/properties-ionic-compounds** `duplicate-labels` - Predict which has the higher melting point, sodium chloride (NaCl) or sodium oxide (Na₂O), and justify your answer using ionic charge.
    - [x] `Sodium oxide — its O²⁻ ions carry a 2− charge compared with Cl⁻ at 1−, giving stronger forces and a higher melting point`
    - [ ] `Sodium chloride — because it contains chlorine, which is heavier than oxygen`
    - [ ] `They are equal, because both contain sodium ions`
    - [ ] `Sodium oxide — because it has more atoms in its formula`

**16. KS4 chemistry/chemical-changes/strong-weak-acids** `duplicate-labels` - Two acids, 0.1 mol/dm³ HCl and 0.1 mol/dm³ CH₃COOH, are compared. Which has a lower pH and why?
    - [x] `HCl — it fully ionises, producing [H⁺] ≈ 0.1 mol/dm³. CH₃COOH only partially ionises, producing far less H⁺.`
    - [ ] `CH₃COOH — weak acids are more reactive and produce more H⁺`
    - [ ] `Both have the same pH — they are the same concentration`
    - [ ] `HCl — HCl molecules are larger and release more H⁺ per molecule`
    - pages: not rendered

**17. KS4 chemistry/chemical-changes/strong-weak-acids** `duplicate-labels` - Is it possible to have a dilute solution of a strong acid?
    - [x] `Yes — strong means fully ionised, dilute means low concentration. A 0.001 mol/dm³ HCl solution is dilute but still a strong acid.`
    - [ ] `No — strong acids must always be concentrated`
    - [ ] `No — diluting an acid makes it weak`
    - [ ] `Yes — but only if a catalyst is added to maintain ionisation`
    - pages: not rendered

**18. KS4 chemistry/energy-changes/bond-energy-calculations** `duplicate-labels` - A reaction has bond breaking energy of 800 kJ/mol and bond forming energy of 1050 kJ/mol. What is ΔH and is the reaction exothermic or endothermic?
    - [x] `ΔH = −250 kJ/mol — exothermic because more energy is released (forming) than absorbed (breaking)`
    - [ ] `ΔH = +250 kJ/mol — endothermic because the products have more energy`
    - [ ] `ΔH = +1850 kJ/mol — add both bond energies together`
    - [ ] `ΔH = −250 kJ/mol — endothermic because the surroundings lose energy`
    - pages: /combined/higher/chemistry/energy-changes/bond-energy-calculations.html, /triple/higher/chemistry/energy-changes/bond-energy-calculations.html

    - [ ] `Endothermic — energy is absorbed, warming the hand from within`
    - [ ] `Neither — hand warmers use electrical energy, not chemical reactions`
    - [ ] `Endothermic — the hand warmer absorbs heat from the hand`

**23. KS4 physics/forces/motion-in-a-circle** `duplicate-labels` - A satellite orbits Earth at constant speed. Is it accelerating? Explain.

    - [ ] `Yes — it accelerates away from Earth to maintain altitude`
    - [ ] `No — acceleration requires a change in speed, which doesn't occur`

**24. KS4 physics/particle-model/particle-motion-pressure** `duplicate-labels` - A gas is heated in a sealed, rigid container, so its volume stays the same. What happens to the pressure of the gas, and why?

    - [ ] `It decreases — the molecules spread out as they get hotter, so fewer of them hit the walls`
    - [ ] `It stays the same — the number of molecules in the container has not changed`
    - [ ] `It increases — the molecules get bigger when heated, so they take up more space`
    - pages: /combined/foundation/physics/particle-model/particle-motion-pressure.html, /combined/higher/physics/particle-model/particle-motion-pressure.html, /triple/foundation/physics/particle-model/particle-motion-pressure.html, /triple/higher/physics/particle-model/particle-motion-pressure.html

**25. KS3 B1/life-processes/apply** `duplicate-labels` - A robot vacuum crosses the floor on its own, senses the stairs and drives to its charger when the battery is low. Which statement is right?

    - [ ] `You would have to watch it for longer to say`
    - [x] `It is not alive — it is not made of cells`
    - pages: /ks3/biology/cells-and-organisation/life-processes.html

**26. KS3 B10/chromosomes-genes-and-dna/apply** `duplicate-labels` - Does a cell in your foot contain the gene for eye colour?
    - [x] `Yes — every cell with a nucleus has the complete set, but only some genes are switched on in each cell`
    - [ ] `No — that gene is only in the cells of the eye, so each cell carries only the genes its organ uses`
    - [ ] `Only if you have brown eyes, because a gene for a characteristic is only present in the people who show it`
    - [ ] `No — genes move to the organs that need them, so a gene ends up in whichever cells are using it`
    - pages: /ks3/biology/inheritance-and-dna/chromosomes-genes-and-dna.html

**27. KS3 B10/passing-it-on-heredity/recall** `duplicate-labels` - A human body cell has 46 chromosomes. How many are in a sperm cell, and why?

    - [ ] `23 — because half the chromosomes are lost when the sperm is made`
    - [ ] `92 — the chromosomes double before fertilisation`
    - [x] `23 — one from each pair, so the fertilised egg has 46 again`
    - pages: /ks3/biology/inheritance-and-dna/passing-it-on-heredity.html

**28. KS3 B3/energy-in-food-and-what-you-need/recall** `duplicate-labels` - A 13-year-old needing 9500 kJ eats: cereal (1100 kJ), two chapatis with dal (1750 kJ), an apple (320 kJ), pasta (2400 kJ) and two chocolate bars (1050 kJ each). What is their intake, and how does it compare?

    - [ ] `7670 kJ — about 1800 kJ more than the requirement`
    - [x] `7670 kJ — about 1800 kJ short of the requirement`
    - [ ] `9500 kJ — the intake always matches the requirement`

**29. KS3 B7/why-almost-all-life-depends-on-it/apply** `duplicate-labels` - A mushroom is not green and cannot photosynthesise. Does it depend on photosynthesis?
    - [ ] `No — an organism that takes its food straight from the compost it grows in is making its own, so it needs no producer`
    - [ ] `No — a fungus takes its energy from the minerals in the soil, so sunlight never enters its chain`
    - [x] `Yes — it feeds on dead material that a photosynthetic organism built, so it sits at the end of the same chain`
    - [ ] `Only if it is growing on a living plant — what is dead has stopped being part of any chain`
    - pages: /ks3/biology/photosynthesis/why-almost-all-life-depends-on-it.html

    - [ ] `Yes — it is the reason the planet is warming, so the less of it the better`
    - [ ] `No — it makes almost no difference to temperature, so adding more of it changes little`
    - [ ] `Yes — human activity produces it, so there was no greenhouse effect before people burned anything`
    - [x] `No — without it the Earth would average about −18 °C. The problem is strengthening it`

    - [ ] `Only if it melts first, because melting is what starts the cycle`
    - [x] `Yes — weathering and erosion can act on any rock at the surface`
    - pages: /ks3/chemistry/the-earth-and-its-atmosphere/the-rock-cycle.html

    - [ ] `Salt: chemical. Marble: physical.`

    - [ ] `The retina and the lens — both bring the light to a point at the back.`
    - [ ] `The iris and the shutter — both open to let a picture through.`
    - [x] `The pupil and the aperture — both set how much light is let in.`
    - [ ] `The optic nerve and the lens — both carry the picture inwards.`
    - pages: /ks3/physics/light/the-eye-and-the-camera.html

## 4. KS4 topic-bank questions, by subject / topic / subtopic

A question that is identical on several routes is listed once, with its routes. `topic page` = the generated page's own Test-yourself quiz; `lesson` = a Design lesson page whose practice reads this question through `KS4.item()`. A question the current build does not show on any page is marked **not rendered**.


### biology / bioenergetics / aerobic-respiration

- **What is the difference between respiration and breathing?** - routes CF/CH/TF/TH - **not rendered** on any current page
    - [x] `Breathing moves air in and out of the lungs. Respiration is the chemical process in cells that releases energy from glucose.` (unchanged)
    - [ ] `They are the same process — both refer to taking in oxygen and releasing carbon dioxide.` -> `They are the same process`  (feedback already authored; reason dropped)
    - [ ] `Breathing produces ATP. Respiration produces oxygen.` (unchanged)
    - [ ] `Respiration only occurs in the lungs. Breathing occurs throughout the body.` (unchanged)

    - [x] `Muscle cells need much more ATP energy for contraction — more mitochondria produce more ATP` -> `Muscle cells need much more ATP energy for contraction`
    - [ ] `Mitochondria are heavier than other organelles and sink to the bottom of active cells` (unchanged)
    - [ ] `Skin cells are too small to contain many mitochondria` (unchanged)
    - [ ] `Muscle cells use more oxygen, so they need more mitochondria to absorb it` (unchanged)

## 5. Inline questions on the Design lesson pages (V() and Q() lists)

These sit inside the lesson page itself, not in `all_subtopics_*.py`, and reach the pupil through the same `KS4.item()` doorway.


### /combined/foundation/biology/ecology/abiotic-biotic-factors.html

- **A river becomes polluted and oxygen levels fall. Which of the following is an abiotic factor that has changed?**
    - [x] `O₂ concentration — a non-living chemical factor that affects aquatic organisms` -> `O₂ concentration`
    - [ ] `Fish population — the fish that die because of low oxygen` -> `Fish population`  (feedback already authored; reason dropped)
    - [ ] `Disease — bacteria cause the oxygen to fall` -> `Disease`  (feedback already authored; reason dropped)
    - [ ] `Competition — fish compete for the remaining oxygen` -> `Competition`  (feedback already authored; reason dropped)
- **Grey squirrels were introduced to the UK and have outcompeted red squirrels. What type of factor is this?**
    - EXCEPTION `duplicate-labels` - left exactly as authored
    - [x] `Biotic — interspecific competition between two different species for food and habitat` (unchanged)
    - [ ] `Abiotic — grey squirrels changed the temperature of the habitat` (unchanged)
    - [ ] `Biotic — intraspecific competition between individuals of the same species` (unchanged)
    - [ ] `Abiotic — grey squirrels are larger so they change the physical environment` (unchanged)

    - [ ] `An ecosystem only includes animals. A community includes all living things.` (unchanged)

    - [x] `pH 2 has 100 times more H⁺ than pH 4 — each unit on the log scale is 10×` -> `pH 2 has 100 times more H⁺ than pH 4`
    - [ ] `pH 2 has twice as many H⁺ ions as pH 4` (unchanged)
    - [ ] `pH 4 has more H⁺ ions — higher pH means more hydrogen` -> `pH 4 has more H⁺ ions`  (feedback already authored; reason dropped)
    - [ ] `They have the same H⁺ concentration — both are acidic` -> `They have the same H⁺ concentration`  (feedback already authored; reason dropped)

### /triple/higher/chemistry/chemical-changes/strong-weak-acids.html

- **Two acids, 0.1 mol/dm³ HCl and 0.1 mol/dm³ CH₃COOH, are compared. Which has a lower pH and why?**
    - EXCEPTION `duplicate-labels` - left exactly as authored
    - [x] `HCl — it fully ionises, producing [H⁺] ≈ 0.1 mol/dm³. CH₃COOH only partially ionises, producing far less H⁺.` (unchanged)
    - [ ] `CH₃COOH — weak acids are more reactive and produce more H⁺` (unchanged)
    - [ ] `Both have the same pH — they are the same concentration` (unchanged)
    - [ ] `HCl — HCl molecules are larger and release more H⁺ per molecule` (unchanged)
- **Is it possible to have a dilute solution of a strong acid?**
    - EXCEPTION `duplicate-labels` - left exactly as authored
    - [x] `Yes — strong means fully ionised, dilute means low concentration. A 0.001 mol/dm³ HCl solution is dilute but still a strong acid.` (unchanged)
    - [ ] `No — strong acids must always be concentrated` (unchanged)
    - [ ] `No — diluting an acid makes it weak` (unchanged)
    - [ ] `Yes — but only if a catalyst is added to maintain ionisation` (unchanged)

### /triple/higher/chemistry/quantitative/amounts-in-equations.html

- **N₂ + 3H₂ → 2NH₃. How many moles of NH₃ are produced from 0.6 mol of H₂?**
    - [x] `0.4 mol — ratio H₂:NH₃ = 3:2, so 0.6 × (2/3) = 0.4 mol` -> `0.4 mol`
    - [ ] `0.6 mol — same moles as H₂ used` -> `0.6 mol`  (feedback already authored; reason dropped)
    - [ ] `0.9 mol — multiply 0.6 by the coefficient 3/2` -> `0.9 mol`  (feedback already authored; reason dropped)
    - [ ] `1.2 mol — double the H₂ moles` -> `1.2 mol`  (feedback already authored; reason dropped)

### /triple/higher/chemistry/quantitative/mass-changes-reactions.html

- **A student heats calcium carbonate in an open crucible. CaCO₃ → CaO + CO₂. What happens to the measured mass?**
    - [x] `The mass decreases — CO₂ gas escapes from the open crucible` -> `The mass decreases`
    - [ ] `The mass increases — oxygen from air is absorbed` -> `The mass increases`  (feedback already authored; reason dropped)
    - [ ] `The mass stays the same — conservation of mass` -> `The mass stays the same`  (feedback already authored; reason dropped)
    - [ ] `The mass first increases, then decreases` (unchanged)
- **Why does the mass of iron increase when it rusts in air?**
    - [x] `Oxygen from the air combines with the iron — the oxygen adds to the mass of the solid product (iron oxide)` -> `Oxygen from the air combines with the iron`
    - [ ] `Water from humidity condenses on the rust — adding extra mass` -> `Water from humidity condenses on the rust`  (feedback already authored; reason dropped)
    - [ ] `The iron becomes denser as it rusts — same volume but more mass` -> `The iron becomes denser as it rusts`  (feedback already authored; reason dropped)
    - [ ] `Iron oxide has more atoms per formula unit than iron` (unchanged)

### /triple/higher/chemistry/quantitative/moles.html

- **How many moles are in 9.8 g of sulfuric acid (H₂SO₄)? Mr = 98.**
    - [x] `0.1 mol — n = 9.8 ÷ 98 = 0.1` -> `0.1 mol`
    - [ ] `10 mol — n = 98 ÷ 9.8 = 10` -> `10 mol`  (feedback already authored; reason dropped)
    - [ ] `960.4 mol — n = 9.8 × 98 = 960.4` -> `960.4 mol`  (feedback already authored; reason dropped)
    - [ ] `0.01 mol — n = 9.8 ÷ 980 = 0.01` -> `0.01 mol`  (feedback already authored; reason dropped)
- **What mass of calcium carbonate (CaCO₃, Mr = 100) contains 3.01 × 10²³ formula units?**
    - [x] `50 g — 3.01 × 10²³ ÷ 6.02 × 10²³ = 0.5 mol; mass = 0.5 × 100 = 50 g` -> `50 g`
    - [ ] `100 g — 1 mole has mass 100 g regardless of particle count` -> `100 g`  (feedback already authored; reason dropped)
    - [ ] `30100 g — multiplying particle count by Mr` -> `30100 g`  (feedback already authored; reason dropped)
    - [ ] `0.5 g — confusing moles with grams` -> `0.5 g`  (feedback already authored; reason dropped)

### B10 / what-makes-a-species - /ks3/biology/inheritance-and-dna/what-makes-a-species.html

- **Two birds mate and produce healthy chicks. The chicks grow up and are unable to have young of their own. Same species?** (recall)

### B11 / biodiversity-and-gene-banks - /ks3/biology/evolution-extinction-and-biodiversity/biodiversity-and-gene-banks.html

- **A conservation charity says a seed bank means a plant species can never truly be lost. What is the strongest objection?** (apply)

### P6 / how-sound-is-made - /ks3/physics/waves-and-sound/how-sound-is-made.html

### P6 / sound-is-longitudinal - /ks3/physics/waves-and-sound/sound-is-longitudinal.html

### P6 / transverse-waves-and-superposition - /ks3/physics/waves-and-sound/transverse-waves-and-superposition.html

    - [ ] `The whole tank goes flat, because the two waves cancel everywhere they are present.` (unchanged)

- **Two waves meet crest on crest. One has an amplitude of 6 mm, the other 4 mm. What is the amplitude of the water where they overlap?** (recall)
    - [ ] `5 mm — take the average, because the surface can only be in one place at a time` -> `5 mm`  (feedback already authored; reason dropped)
    - [x] `10 mm — add the two, because both waves lift the surface at the same moment` -> `10 mm`
    - [ ] `24 mm — multiply the two amplitudes, because both waves are acting at once` -> `24 mm`  (feedback already authored; reason dropped)
    - [ ] `2 mm — take the smaller from the bigger, because one wave always cancels part of the other` -> `2 mm`  (feedback already authored; reason dropped)

### P8 / conductors-and-insulators - /ks3/physics/electric-circuits/conductors-and-insulators.html

- **A student clips a plastic ruler across 6 V, sees the ammeter stay on zero, and concludes that the resistance of plastic is infinite. What is right?** (apply)
    - [ ] `The student is right: an insulator has infinite resistance, which is why the reading is exactly zero — a material either has free charges in it and conducts, or has none at all and stops the current dead.` -> `The student is right: an insulator has infinite resistance, which is why the reading is exactly zero`  (feedback already authored; reason dropped)
    - [ ] `The resistance cannot be worked out at all, because dividing by a zero reading is impossible.` (unchanged)
    - [ ] `The resistance is enormous, because plastic has no atoms that can carry charge.` (unchanged)
    - [x] `The resistance is enormous but finite — around 2 TΩ for that ruler, which passes a current of a few millionths of a millionth of an amp, far below what the meter can show.` -> `The resistance is enormous but finite`
- **A specimen is clipped across a 6.0 V supply and the ammeter reads 0.15 A. What is its resistance, and how would you classify it?** (recall)
    - EXCEPTION `duplicate-labels` - left exactly as authored
    - [ ] `0.9 Ω — a conductor` (unchanged)
    - [x] `40 Ω — a conductor` (unchanged)
    - [ ] `40 Ω — an insulator` (unchanged)
    - [ ] `0.025 Ω — a very good conductor` (unchanged)

### P8 / current-and-circuits - /ks3/physics/electric-circuits/current-and-circuits.html

- **A student wires a lamp on a very long cable, then says the light will come on a moment late because the electricity has to travel down the wire first. What is wrong with that?** (apply)
    - [x] `The wire is already full of free electrons, so closing the switch pushes on all of them at once and the far end starts moving almost immediately.` (unchanged)
    - [ ] `Nothing is wrong — there is a delay, but it is too small to notice because electricity travels at the speed of light.` -> `Nothing is wrong`  (feedback already authored; reason dropped)
    - [ ] `The student is right, and on a cable a few kilometres long you would see the lamp light up noticeably late.` (unchanged)
    - [ ] `There is no delay because a cell does not send electrons out — it makes new ones in the wire as they are needed.` -> `There is no delay because a cell does not send electrons out`  (feedback already authored; reason dropped)
- **A single loop holds a cell, a switch, a bulb and two ammeters, one either side of the bulb. The first reads 0.24 A. What does the second read?** (recall)
    - [ ] `0.12 A — the bulb uses up about half of it` -> `0.12 A`  (feedback already authored; reason dropped)
    - [ ] `0 A — the current has already been spent on the bulb` -> `0 A`  (feedback already authored; reason dropped)
    - [x] `0.24 A — the current is the same everywhere in a single loop` -> `0.24 A`
    - [ ] `0.48 A — the bulb pushes the current on and it speeds up` -> `0.48 A`  (feedback already authored; reason dropped)

### P8 / current-at-a-junction - /ks3/physics/electric-circuits/current-at-a-junction.html

- **Two lamps are in parallel on a battery, each drawing 0.30 A. A student unscrews one and predicts the other will now draw 0.60 A, because it gets all the current to itself. What is right?** (apply)
    - [ ] `The student is right — the total from the battery is fixed at 0.60 A, so the remaining lamp must take all of it.` -> `The student is right`  (feedback already authored; reason dropped)
    - [ ] `It draws 0.15 A, because removing a branch makes the circuit harder to get round.` (unchanged)
    - [x] `It still draws 0.30 A. Its branch has the same battery across it as before, and the main wire now carries 0.30 A instead of 0.60 A.` (unchanged)
    - [ ] `It still draws 0.30 A, because a battery always gives out the same current whatever is connected to it.` (unchanged)
- **A junction splits into three branches. The main wire carries 0.85 A, the first branch 0.30 A and the second 0.40 A. What does the third branch carry?** (recall)
    - [x] `0.15 A` (unchanged)
    - [ ] `1.55 A — add all three readings together` -> `1.55 A`  (feedback already authored; reason dropped)
    - [ ] `0.28 A — share the main current equally between the three branches` -> `0.28 A`  (feedback already authored; reason dropped)
    - [ ] `0.10 A — take the smaller branch away from the larger one` -> `0.10 A`  (feedback already authored; reason dropped)

### P8 / potential-difference - /ks3/physics/electric-circuits/potential-difference.html

- **A student measures 3.0 V across a battery and 3.0 V across the single lamp in its loop, and concludes the meter must be broken because the voltage should have been used up. What is right?** (apply)
    - [ ] `The student is right — the reading across the lamp should be smaller, because the lamp uses some of the voltage up on its way round, so what is left over is what the voltmeter across the battery is showing.` -> `The student is right`  (feedback already authored; reason dropped)
    - [x] `Both readings are correct. With one component in the loop it takes the whole share, so the p.d. across it equals the p.d. across the battery.` (unchanged)
    - [ ] `The reading across the lamp should be 1.5 V, because half the p.d. is lost in the connecting wires.` (unchanged)
    - [ ] `Both readings are correct, because a voltmeter always reads the battery’s value wherever you put it.` (unchanged)
- **Three components sit in series on a 6.0 V battery. Voltmeters across the first two read 1.5 V and 3.0 V. What is the p.d. across the third?** (recall)
    - [ ] `10.5 V — add all three readings together` -> `10.5 V`  (feedback already authored; reason dropped)
    - [ ] `2.0 V — share the 6.0 V equally between the three` -> `2.0 V`  (feedback already authored; reason dropped)
    - [ ] `6.0 V — every component in a loop gets the battery’s full p.d.` -> `6.0 V`  (feedback already authored; reason dropped)
    - [x] `1.5 V` (unchanged)

### P8 / resistance - /ks3/physics/electric-circuits/resistance.html

- **A student tests a filament lamp at 2 V and gets 6.4 Ω, then at 10 V and gets 16.0 Ω, and decides one of the measurements must be a mistake. What is right?** (apply)
    - [x] `Both are right. The filament is hotter at 10 V, and hot metal resists more — so the lamp genuinely has a different resistance in each state.` -> `Both are right. The filament is hotter at 10 V, and hot metal resists more`
    - [ ] `The 6.4 Ω reading is the real resistance, and the 16.0 Ω reading is wrong because the meters are affected by the heat coming off the filament, so a reading taken while a lamp is glowing is never the component's own value.` (unchanged)
    - [ ] `The student is right — R = V ÷ I is a law, so a component can only have one resistance.` -> `The student is right`  (feedback already authored; reason dropped)
    - [ ] `Both are right, because the resistance of any component always goes up when you turn the supply up.` (unchanged)
- **A voltmeter across a wire reads 4.5 V and the ammeter in the loop reads 0.90 A. What is the resistance of the wire?** (recall)
    - [ ] `4.05 Ω — multiply the two readings` -> `4.05 Ω`  (feedback already authored; reason dropped)
    - [ ] `0.2 Ω — divide the current by the p.d.` -> `0.2 Ω`  (feedback already authored; reason dropped)
    - [x] `5.0 Ω` (unchanged)
    - [ ] `5.0 V — the number is the p.d. needed for one amp` -> `5.0 V`  (feedback already authored; reason dropped)

### P8 / series-and-parallel - /ks3/physics/electric-circuits/series-and-parallel.html

- **A student adds a second identical bulb in series with the first and predicts each bulb will be as bright as before, because the battery has not changed. What is wrong?** (apply)
    - [ ] `Nothing is wrong — in series the current is the same everywhere, so both bulbs must be as bright as the single bulb was, because a bulb's brightness is set by the current passing through it and that current has not changed anywhere in the loop.` -> `Nothing is wrong`  (feedback already authored; reason dropped)
    - [ ] `The first bulb will be as bright as before and the second will be dim, because the current reaches the first bulb first.` (unchanged)
    - [ ] `The bulbs will be dimmer because the battery has to divide its charge between two bulbs and each gets half as much.` (unchanged)
    - [x] `A second bulb in series makes it harder for the charge to get round the one loop, so the current everywhere falls — here from 0.30 A to 0.15 A — and both bulbs are dim.` -> `A second bulb in series makes it harder for the charge to get round the one loop, so the current everywhere falls`

### P9 / charging-by-rubbing - /ks3/physics/static-electricity/charging-by-rubbing.html

- **A student rubs a balloon on a jumper, finds the balloon negative, and says the friction created the negative charge. What is right?** (apply)
    - [ ] `The student is right — friction turns movement into electric charge, which is why you have to rub hard.` -> `The student is right`  (feedback already authored; reason dropped)
    - [ ] `No charge was created, because the balloon and the jumper end up with the same charge as each other.` (unchanged)
    - [x] `No charge was created. Electrons moved from the jumper to the balloon, so the jumper is now positive by exactly as much as the balloon is negative.` (unchanged)
    - [ ] `Charge was created, but an equal amount of positive charge was created on the jumper at the same time, so the two new charges balance and the total stays zero.` (unchanged)

### P9 / electric-fields - /ks3/physics/static-electricity/electric-fields.html

- **A student says the field between two equal positive charges must be at its strongest half-way between them, because that point is close to both. What is right?** (apply)
    - [ ] `The student is right — being close to two charges instead of one makes it the strongest point on the map.` -> `The student is right`  (feedback already authored; reason dropped)
    - [x] `It is zero there. The two charges push a positive test charge in exactly opposite directions with equal strength, so the pushes cancel.` (unchanged)
    - [ ] `It is zero there, because the two charges cancel each other out everywhere between them.` (unchanged)
    - [ ] `It is half the strength of a single charge at the same distance, because the two contributions are averaged.` (unchanged)

### P9 / forces-between-charges - /ks3/physics/static-electricity/forces-between-charges.html

- **A negatively charged rod is held near a hanging metal-coated ball on a thread. The ball swings towards the rod. What can you conclude about the ball?** (recall)
    - [ ] `It must be positively charged, because unlike charges attract` (unchanged)
    - [x] `Nothing certain — it could be positively charged, or it could be neutral` -> `Nothing certain`
    - [ ] `It must be neutral, because a charged ball would have been pushed away` (unchanged)
    - [ ] `It must be negatively charged, because the rod is negative` (unchanged)

- `Wrong — it hardly dissolved, so nothing was in the water to read`
- `Right — copper oxide is neutral, the same as water is`
- `Wrong — copper oxide is really an acidic oxide`

- `Right — the yellow flame is hotter; the brightness is heat you can see`
- `Wrong — the blue flame is hotter; the yellow glow is glowing soot`

- `Wrong — both flames are the same temperature; only the size has changed`

- `Wrong — the powder only looks black while it is hot`

## 8. Parked follow-up (out of scope, Mide 17 Oct 2026): colon tails

Authored `Q()` items on the Design batch pages put the reason or working after a COLON instead of an em-dash (`100 mol: 2.7 kg = 2700 g, and 2700 / 27 = 100`). Same giveaway, outside the ruling, NOT changed. Questions whose correct option carries a colon tail:

- /combined/foundation/biology/ecology/deforestation.html - **A forest is cleared to grow a biofuel crop. Give one benefit and one cost.** -> correct: `Benefit: fuel to replace some fossil fuel. Cost: carbon dioxide released and habitat lost`
- /combined/foundation/biology/ecology/food-chains-webs.html - **A pupil writes the chain: fox, rabbit, grass, with arrows pointing from fox to rabbit to grass. What is wrong?** -> correct: `The arrows point the wrong way: they should point from grass to rabbit to fox`
- /combined/foundation/biology/ecology/food-chains-webs.html - **In the web, shrews are eaten by owls and foxes, and shrews eat grasshoppers. If the shrews die out, what happens to the owls?** -> correct: `They fall: they have lost a food source`
- /combined/foundation/biology/homeostasis/blood-glucose-diabetes.html - **What is the key difference between Type 1 and Type 2 diabetes?** -> correct: `Type 1: the pancreas fails to produce sufficient insulin. Type 2: the body cells no longer respond to the insulin produced by the pancreas.`
- /combined/foundation/biology/homeostasis/blood-glucose-diabetes.html - **Made-up data: after a glucose drink, Person A’s blood glucose rises to 140 and is back to normal in 2 hours. Person B’s rises to 260 and is still high after 3 hours. What does this suggest?** -> correct: `Person B has diabetes: not enough insulin effect to bring the glucose down`
- /combined/foundation/biology/homeostasis/endocrine-system.html - **A diagram shows: thyroxine level falls, the pituitary releases more TSH, the thyroid releases more thyroxine. What happens next?** -> correct: `The thyroxine level rises back to normal: negative feedback`
- /combined/foundation/biology/inheritance/meiosis.html - **Where in the human body does meiosis happen?** -> correct: `In the reproductive organs: testes and ovaries`
- /combined/foundation/chemistry/atomic-structure/electronic-structure.html - **Helium is 2. Why is it in Group 0 rather than Group 2?** -> correct: `Its outer shell is full: the first shell holds only 2`
- /combined/foundation/chemistry/atomic-structure/group-7.html - **Chlorine reacts with hydrogen to form hydrogen chloride. What type of compound is hydrogen chloride?** -> correct: `A covalent molecular compound: a halogen with a non-metal`
- /combined/foundation/chemistry/chemical-changes/oxidation-reduction.html - **Iron(III) oxide is heated with carbon: 2Fe₂O₃ + 3C → 4Fe + 3CO₂. What happens to the iron(III) oxide?** -> correct: `It is reduced: it loses oxygen`
- /combined/foundation/chemistry/chemical-changes/oxidation-reduction.html - **Magnesium burns in air: 2Mg + O₂ → 2MgO. Which word describes what happens to the magnesium?** -> correct: `Oxidised: it gains oxygen`
- /combined/foundation/chemistry/chemical-changes/oxidation-reduction.html - **Zinc is added to copper sulfate solution: Zn + Cu²⁺ → Zn²⁺ + Cu. Which species is oxidised?** -> correct: `Zinc: it loses two electrons to form Zn²⁺`
- /combined/foundation/chemistry/chemical-changes/salts-neutralisation.html - **Why is copper oxide added to the acid until some is left undissolved?** -> correct: `So that all the acid reacts: none is left to contaminate the salt`
- /combined/foundation/chemistry/quantitative/mass-changes-reactions.html - **The same reaction is run in a sealed flask. What happens to the balance reading?** -> correct: `It stays the same: the gas is made but cannot leave`
- /combined/foundation/chemistry/quantitative/mass-changes-reactions.html - **0.2 mol of magnesium burns: 2Mg + O₂ → 2MgO. How many moles of oxygen molecules react?** -> correct: `0.1 mol: the ratio Mg : O₂ is 2 : 1`
- /combined/higher/chemistry/chemical-changes/strong-weak-acids.html - **Solution A has pH 1 and solution B has pH 4. How many times greater is the hydrogen ion concentration in A?** -> correct: `1000 times: three units, 10³`
- /combined/higher/chemistry/quantitative/amounts-in-equations.html - **Calculate the mass of magnesium oxide formed when 4.8 g of magnesium burns completely. 2Mg + O₂ → 2MgO. Ar: Mg = 24, O = 16.** -> correct: `8.0 g: n(Mg) = 0.2 mol; ratio 2 : 2 gives 0.2 mol MgO; 0.2 × 40 = 8.0 g`
- /combined/higher/chemistry/quantitative/amounts-in-equations.html - **Mg + 2HCl → MgCl₂ + H₂. What is the Mr of hydrogen gas used in Step 3?** -> correct: `2: hydrogen gas is H₂`
- /combined/higher/chemistry/quantitative/moles.html - **How many moles of aluminium atoms are in 2.7 kg of aluminium? Ar(Al) = 27.** -> correct: `100 mol: 2.7 kg = 2700 g, and 2700 ÷ 27 = 100`
- /combined/higher/chemistry/quantitative/moles.html - **How many molecules are there in 36 g of water? Mr(H₂O) = 18.** -> correct: `1.204 × 10²⁴: 36 ÷ 18 = 2 mol, then 2 × 6.02 × 10²³`
- /combined/higher/chemistry/quantitative/moles.html - **One mole of water, H₂O, contains 6.02 × 10²³ molecules. How many atoms does it contain?** -> correct: `3 × 6.02 × 10²³ = 1.806 × 10²⁴: each molecule has three atoms`
- /triple/foundation/biology/ecology/role-of-biotechnology.html - **What is the intended benefit of golden rice?** -> correct: `Improved nutritional value: it makes beta-carotene, a source of vitamin A`
- /triple/foundation/biology/ecology/transfer-of-biomass.html - **Producers have a biomass of 5000 g/m². Primary consumers have a biomass of 500 g/m². What is the efficiency of transfer?** -> correct: `10%: (500 ÷ 5000) × 100`
- /triple/foundation/biology/ecology/transfer-of-biomass.html - **Grain 10 000 kg, cattle fed on it 1000 kg, people eating the beef 100 kg. What percentage of the grain’s biomass reaches the people?** -> correct: `1%: 10% of 10%`
- /triple/foundation/chemistry/atomic-structure/transition-metals.html - **Copper(II) sulfate solution is blue and sodium sulfate solution is colourless. What does this show about copper?** -> correct: `Copper is a transition metal: transition-metal compounds are often coloured`
- /triple/higher/biology/ecology/environmental-change.html - **Information: oak trees now come into leaf about 10 days earlier than 40 years ago. Caterpillars that eat the new leaves hatch earlier too. A bird that feeds its chicks on these caterpillars has not changed its nesting time. Suggest the effect on the birds.** -> correct: `Fewer caterpillars when the chicks hatch, so fewer chicks survive: a timing mismatch`

Total: 26 questions.
